But yeah, no, I don't repeat behaviors or anything.\n\nDoctor: Are your worries mostly about one thing, like work or school, or are they spread across many different areas of your life?\n\nPatient: Oh, it's definitely spread out. My mind is just constantly jumping around from one thing to the next. Like, I'll be worrying about whether our lab's new partnership is going to end up cutting my position. Which, speaking of that partnership, the corporate reps from the other company came in for a tour on Thursday, and one of them was wearing these ridiculous suede loafers. In a wet lab! Suede! Do you know how porous suede is? If someone spilled even a drop of the toluene we were working with, it would soak right through to his skin in a fraction of a second. I tried to point out the safety hazard to my supervisor, but he just shushed me because they're the ones bringing in the funding. It's completely insane to ignore basic OSHA protocols just because a guy has a nice watch. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, I'll be worrying about losing my job to that merger, and then suddenly my brain switches to agonizing over whether my data from the morning batches are wrong. And then I think, if my data is wrong and I lose my job, my bills are going to pile up. I just got my electric bill yesterday and they raised the delivery fee again, which is a total scam. They don't even explain what the delivery fee actually covers on the statement, it's just a flat rate that keeps going up. So I'm stressing about the bills, and then that makes me think about how my family already thinks I'm falling behind everyone else my age, and I start panicking about whether I've already completely ruined my chances at ever getting into grad school. \n\nSo, no, it's not just work or school. It's... it's literally everything. It never really stops.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is interfering with your ability to enjoy daily activities or function normally?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I... I guess it is. It's definitely getting in the way of things. Like, I completely missed an internal lab deadline last week because I just couldn't get my writing and my data review organized. My brain was just buzzing too much. Which, speaking of the data review, the new software they made us install for the spectroscopy analysis is an absolute nightmare. The user interface looks like it was designed in 1998, and it keeps crashing every time I try to export the raw files to Excel. I put in a ticket with IT three days ago, and you know what they told me? They said to \"try restarting the computer.\" As if I haven't tried that a dozen times already! It's infuriating. They get paid to sit in that air-conditioned basement and they can't even troubleshoot a basic compatibility issue. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, missing that deadline was bad. And now it feels like even just the regular, everyday stuff is too much. Preparing the routine reports, showing up for these weekly art nights my friends do, even just cooking a meal for my family... it all just feels like this massive mountain I can't climb. I'm just too exhausted from worrying all the time to actually keep up with any of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is causing you significant distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it really is. I mean, like I was just saying, it's causing so much distress that I'm literally dropping the ball on everything. Missing that internal lab deadline was a huge deal. I just couldn't get my writing and my data review organized because my head was spinning. Which, honestly, part of the reason I couldn't get it organized is because my desk is right next to the main walkway in the lab, and people are constantly walking by dragging their feet. There's this one guy, Dave, from the synthesis team, and he wears these heavy work boots that scuff against the linoleum every single time he takes a step. *Scuff, scuff, scuff.* It's maddening. I asked him once if he could maybe pick his feet up a little, and he looked at me like I was the crazy one. But how are you supposed to concentrate on complex data review when someone is essentially sandpapering the floor right behind your head? \n\nBut anyway, yeah, the distress is just... it's overwhelming. It's not just the big deadlines, either. It's gotten to the point where even routine responsibilities feel impossible. Like preparing the standard weekly reports, or showing up for those art nights my friends do on Tuesdays, or even just cooking a decent meal for my family. It all just feels like way too much to keep up with. I'm just so drained by the constant worrying that I can't handle the normal stuff anymore.\n\nDoctor: Is there any part of your life where you feel your worrying is less or not present at all?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don't think there's anywhere it just... shuts off. Even when I try to force myself to take a break, it doesn't work. Like, yesterday I went into the staff breakroom just to sit and stare at the wall for five minutes and try to clear my head. But as soon as I sat down, I noticed someone had left the microwave door wide open. Which, first of all, leaves the little interior light on, and that just wastes the bulb's lifespan for literally no reason. And second, I could see splattered red sauce all over the ceiling of the inside. Who microwaves pasta without putting a paper towel over the bowl? It's basic thermodynamics! The water in the sauce heats up, expands into steam, and pops the food everywhere. It's so completely disrespectful to a communal space. \n\nI ended up spending my whole quote-unquote break scrubbing baked-on marinara off the rotating glass plate because I couldn't stand looking at it. And then I started stressing that we're going to get ants in the breakroom, which would be an absolute disaster considering we work in a lab setting where cross-contamination is a huge liability. \n\nSo... yeah. To answer your question, no. I'm always on edge. There isn't really a time or place where the worrying isn't there.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding that your worrying is affecting your relationships with friends and family?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean, I think I mentioned this a minute ago, but it really is. Like I was saying, I've just been too overwhelmed to even cook a basic meal for my family, and I've completely stopped showing up for those Tuesday art nights my friends put together. Which, honestly, maybe it's for the best because the last time I went, somebody brought these incredibly cheap acrylic paints. I'm talking bottom-of-the-barrel, discount-store stuff. The pigment separation was just atrocious. As someone who actually studies materials, looking at that emulsion breakdown just drove me completely insane. You can't just dump tap water into a polymer binder and expect it to adhere to a canvas without flaking! There's a specific cross-linking process that has to happen for the film to form properly. I tried explaining the basic chemical properties to them, but they just wanted to paint their little poorly-blended sunsets. It's just so frustrating when people don't respect the materials they're working with. \n\nBut... wait, what was I saying? Oh, right, my relationships. Yeah, I just can't keep up with them right now. It all feels like too much. Just like how I missed that internal lab deadline because I couldn't get my writing and data review organized. I'm just dropping the ball on everything—my friends, my family, even routine stuff like preparing those standard lab reports. I just can't keep up with any of it anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any physical symptoms like headaches, stomachaches, or difficulty sleeping because of your worrying?\n\nPatient: Headaches or stomachaches? Um, no, I don't think so. I haven't really noticed anything like that. And sleeping... I mean, I guess I sleep okay? Honestly, the only thing that really messes with my sleep is the HVAC unit outside my apartment window. \n\nIt vibrates at this incredibly specific low frequency, like right around 60 hertz, which is just the exact pitch to resonate perfectly with my single-pane glass window. It's a basic acoustic coupling issue. If the property management company would just install a proper elastomeric isolation pad under the condenser unit, it would dampen the vibration completely. I even printed out a spec sheet for a neoprene mount that costs literally twelve dollars and taped it to the manager's door, but they just totally ignored it. It's just willful ignorance of basic physics. You can't just bolt a heavy, unbalanced rotary compressor directly to a concrete slab next to a residential wall and expect the kinetic energy to just magically disappear into the ether. It has to transfer somewhere! It just drives me crazy when people refuse to implement simple, structurally sound solutions. \n\nBut... wait, sorry. What was the question? Oh, right, physical stuff from the worrying. Yeah, no, I don't really get stomachaches or headaches or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding that your worrying is significantly impacting your ability to perform your job or maintain your role at the lab?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... I think I brought this up a few minutes ago, actually. It really is impacting my job. Like I said, I completely missed an internal lab deadline recently because I just couldn't get my writing and data review organized. And it's not even just the big deadlines, it's the routine stuff, too. Even just preparing standard lab reports feels completely overwhelming right now. \n\nWhich is ridiculous because the reporting software we use is supposed to streamline the process, but the user interface is an absolute nightmare. The data visualization module defaults to these absurd, neon color palettes that completely distort the visual hierarchy of the graphs. Like, you can't just put a high-saturation yellow next to a bright cyan on a scatter plot and expect the human eye to accurately process the data point density! It creates this optical illusion called simultaneous contrast where the colors vibrate against each other, making the data look completely skewed. I submitted a ticket to IT explaining how they could just change the default CSS to a colorblind-friendly, sequential palette, which would take them maybe five minutes of coding. But of course, they closed the ticket without even reading it. It's just so infuriating when people don't understand basic visual processing principles. \n\nBut, um... sorry. To get back to your question... yes. The worrying is definitely impacting my job. Between missing that deadline and struggling with the reports... and like I said before, even stuff outside of work, like cooking for my family or going to those art nights. It all just feels like way too much to keep up with.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding that your worrying is making it difficult for you to make decisions or take actions in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Making decisions? Um, no, I don't really think so. I haven't noticed any issues with that. Like, I just had to decide on a new dish soap at the store yesterday, and I just picked one out. \n\nWell, I say I just picked one, but honestly, looking at the options was infuriating because the commercial surfactant blends they use now are completely illogical. They market all these \"eco-friendly\" formulas, but if you actually look at the molecular structure of the primary cleansing agents, they've just substituted standard sulfates with a decyl glucoside that doesn't even have the proper hydrophilic-lipophilic balance to break down basic cooking oils. You end up having to use three times as much product to achieve the same micelle formation! Which completely negates any environmental benefit because you're just dumping massive amounts of excess non-ionic surfactants into the municipal wastewater system. It's just blatant greenwashing masking incredibly lazy organic chemistry. I literally had to stand in the aisle pulling up safety data sheets on my phone because I refuse to buy a product formulated by someone who doesn't understand basic lipid emulsion. \n\nBut... wait, what did you ask me? Oh, right, struggling to make decisions. Yeah, no, I don't really have a problem with that. I usually just figure out what needs to be done and do it.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding that your worrying is making it hard for you to keep up with your responsibilities or maintain relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... I feel like we just talked about this, right? But yes, it really is. Like I said a minute ago, I completely missed an internal lab deadline recently. I just couldn't get my writing and data review organized at all. And it's bleeding into everything else. Even routine stuff, like preparing those lab reports, or trying to cook a decent meal for my family, or just showing up for those art nights I usually go to... it all just feels like way too much to keep up with. \n\nWhich is frustrating about the cooking, especially, because I actually really enjoy the Maillard reaction when you get a perfect sear on a piece of protein. But lately, the quality of the cookware I've been using has been driving me insane. I bought this supposedly \"high-end\" stainless steel skillet, but the thermal conductivity is completely uneven. They claim it has an aluminum core, but if you look at the heat distribution patterns when you deglaze the pan, it's obvious the cladding is way too thin on the edges. You end up with these massive hot spots right in the center, which completely ruins the temperature control needed for proper protein denaturation. It's like the manufacturer didn't even consult a basic thermodynamics textbook before putting it into production. I actually wrote a very detailed review explaining how they could improve the heat capacity by just increasing the thickness of the aluminum layer by a few millimeters, but I'm sure it just got buried under a bunch of fake five-star reviews. People just don't care about structural integrity anymore. \n\nBut, um... yeah. To answer your question... keeping up with things is definitely really hard right now. Between work and home, I just feel like I'm dropping the ball on everything.\n\nDoctor: Is there any aspect of your life where you feel completely free from worrying?\n\nPatient: Um, completely free from it? No, I don't... I don't think so. It's pretty much always there, buzzing in the background, you know? Even when I'm trying to relax, it just creeps in. \n\nLike, I tried to unwind last weekend by setting up my new aquarium. I thought, okay, fish are supposed to be calming, right? They advertised it as a multi-stage biological filter, but the flow rate through the ceramic media is way too fast for any meaningful colonization of nitrifying bacteria. The residence time of the water in the filter chamber is so short that the ammonia conversion rate is practically negligible. And the impeller design! It's supposed to be magnetic drive, but the ceramic shaft has this microscopic wobble that creates a low-frequency hum that resonates through the entire glass tank. It's a basic mechanical engineering failure—if they had just used a slightly denser polymer for the housing or added a silicone dampening ring, it would have eliminated the vibration entirely. I ended up spending six hours reverse-engineering the pump housing and 3D printing a custom baffle just to get the flow dynamics right. It's just exhausting that you can't buy a single consumer product that actually adheres to basic fluid dynamics principles. \n\nBut, uh, sorry, what was the question? Oh, being free from worrying. Yeah, no, I don't really have any part of my life where it just turns off. It's pretty constant.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.   {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Avoid places? Um, no, not really. I don't think I avoid going anywhere specific because of it. Like, I still go out to cafes and stuff to try and get work done. 

Though, I went to this new coffee shop yesterday, and I was absolutely appalled by the takeaway cups they were using. They had these cheap extruded polystyrene lids, which is just... I mean, it's completely irresponsible. When exposed to aqueous solutions above 85 degrees Celsius, the structural integrity of that specific polymer matrix starts to degrade. You risk leaching styrene monomers directly into the beverage! They had a little sign claiming it was high-impact polystyrene, but the glass transition temperature is clearly way too low for the thermal load of a freshly brewed Americano. I was sitting there, and I lightly scraped the inside of the lid with a stirrer, and you could literally feel how the heat had compromised the polymer chains. I actually ended up trying to explain to the manager that they need to switch to a polypropylene or at least a crystallized PET lid if they want to prevent the mass transfer of volatile organic compounds into their customers' drinks. It's basic polymer science, but they just looked at me like I was speaking a different language. It drives me crazy how little thought goes into food-safe material selection.

But, um... sorry, I lost my train of thought. Avoiding places? Yeah, no, I don't avoid anywhere. I just kind of deal with the worrying wherever I am.

I don't think there's anywhere it just... shuts off. Even when I try to force myself to take a break, it doesn't work. Like, yesterday I went into the staff breakroom just to sit and stare at the wall for five minutes and try to clear my head. But as soon as I sat down, I noticed someone had left the microwave door wide open. Which, first of all, leaves the little interior light on, and that just wastes the bulb's lifespan for literally no reason. And second, I could see splattered red sauce all over the ceiling of the inside. Who microwaves pasta without putting a paper towel over the bowl? It's basic thermodynamics! The water in the sauce heats up, expands into steam, and pops the food everywhere. It's so completely disrespectful to a communal space. \n\nI ended up spending my whole quote-unquote break scrubbing baked-on marinara off the rotating glass plate because I couldn't stand looking at it. And then I started stressing that we're going to get ants in the breakroom, which would be an absolute disaster considering we work in a lab setting where cross-contamination is a huge liability. \n\nSo... yeah. To answer your question, no. I'm always on edge. There isn't really a time or place where the worrying isn't there.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding that your worrying is affecting your relationships with friends and family?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean, I think I mentioned this a minute ago, but it really is. Like I was saying, I've just been too overwhelmed to even cook a basic meal for my family, and I've completely stopped showing up for those Tuesday art nights my friends put together. Which, honestly, maybe it's for the best because the last time I went, somebody brought these incredibly cheap acrylic paints. I'm talking bottom-of-the-barrel, discount-store stuff. The pigment separation was just atrocious. As someone who actually studies materials, looking at that emulsion breakdown just drove me completely insane. You can't just dump tap water into a polymer binder and expect it to adhere to a canvas without flaking! There's a specific cross-linking process that has to happen for the film to form properly. I tried explaining the basic chemical properties to them, but they just wanted to paint their little poorly-blended sunsets. It's just so frustrating when people don't respect the materials they're working with. \n\nBut... wait, what was I saying? Oh, right, my relationships. Yeah, I just can't keep up with them right now. It all feels like too much. Just like how I missed that internal lab deadline because I couldn't get my writing and data review organized. I'm just dropping the ball on everything—my friends, my family, even routine stuff like preparing those standard lab reports. I just can't keep up with any of it anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any physical symptoms like headaches, stomachaches, or difficulty sleeping because of your worrying?\n\nPatient: Headaches or stomachaches? Um, no, I don't think so. I haven't really noticed anything like that. And sleeping... I mean, I guess I sleep okay? Honestly, the only thing that really messes with my sleep is the HVAC unit outside my apartment window. \n\nIt vibrates at this incredibly specific low frequency, like right around 60 hertz, which is just the exact pitch to resonate perfectly with my single-pane glass window. It's a basic acoustic coupling issue. If the property management company would just install a proper elastomeric isolation pad under the condenser unit, it would dampen the vibration completely. I even printed out a spec sheet for a neoprene mount that costs literally twelve dollars and taped it to the manager's door, but they just totally ignored it. It's just willful ignorance of basic physics. You can't just bolt a heavy, unbalanced rotary compressor directly to a concrete slab next to a residential wall and expect the kinetic energy to just magically disappear into the ether. It has to transfer somewhere! It just drives me crazy when people refuse to implement simple, structurally sound solutions. \n\nBut... wait, sorry. What was the question? Oh, right, physical stuff from the worrying. Yeah, no, I don't really get stomachaches or headaches or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding that your worrying is significantly impacting your ability to perform your job or maintain your role at the lab?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... I think I brought this up a few minutes ago, actually. It really is impacting my job. Like I said, I completely missed an internal lab deadline recently because I just couldn't get my writing and data review organized. And it's not even just the big deadlines, it's the routine stuff, too. Even just preparing standard lab reports feels completely overwhelming right now. \n\nWhich is ridiculous because the reporting software we use is supposed to streamline the process, but the user interface is an absolute nightmare. The data visualization module defaults to these absurd, neon color palettes that completely distort the visual hierarchy of the graphs. Like, you can't just put a high-saturation yellow next to a bright cyan on a scatter plot and expect the human eye to accurately process the data point density! It creates this optical illusion called simultaneous contrast where the colors vibrate against each other, making the data look completely skewed. I submitted a ticket to IT explaining how they could just change the default CSS to a colorblind-friendly, sequential palette, which would take them maybe five minutes of coding. But of course, they closed the ticket without even reading it. It's just so infuriating when people don't understand basic visual processing principles. \n\nBut, um... sorry. To get back to your question... yes. The worrying is definitely impacting my job. Between missing that deadline and struggling with the reports... and like I said before, even stuff outside of work, like cooking for my family or going to those art nights. It all just feels like way too much to keep up with.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding that your worrying is making it difficult for you to make decisions or take actions in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Making decisions? Um, no, I don't really think so. I haven't noticed any issues with that. Like, I just had to decide on a new dish soap at the store yesterday, and I just picked one out. \n\nWell, I say I just picked one, but honestly, looking at the options was infuriating because the commercial surfactant blends they use now are completely illogical. They market all these \"eco-friendly\" formulas, but if you actually look at the molecular structure of the primary cleansing agents, they've just substituted standard sulfates with a decyl glucoside that doesn't even have the proper hydrophilic-lipophilic balance to break down basic cooking oils. You end up having to use three times as much product to achieve the same micelle formation! Which completely negates any environmental benefit because you're just dumping massive amounts of excess non-ionic surfactants into the municipal wastewater system. It's just blatant greenwashing masking incredibly lazy organic chemistry. I literally had to stand in the aisle pulling up safety data sheets on my phone because I refuse to buy a product formulated by someone who doesn't understand basic lipid emulsion. \n\nBut... wait, what did you ask me? Oh, right, struggling to make decisions. Yeah, no, I don't really have a problem with that. I usually just figure out what needs to be done and do it.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding that your worrying is making it hard for you to keep up with your responsibilities or maintain relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... I feel like we just talked about this, right? But yes, it really is. Like I said a minute ago, I completely missed an internal lab deadline recently. I just couldn't get my writing and data review organized at all. And it's bleeding into everything else. Even routine stuff, like preparing those lab reports, or trying to cook a decent meal for my family, or just showing up for those art nights I usually go to... it all just feels like way too much to keep up with. \n\nWhich is frustrating about the cooking, especially, because I actually really enjoy the Maillard reaction when you get a perfect sear on a piece of protein. But lately, the quality of the cookware I've been using has been driving me insane. I bought this supposedly \"high-end\" stainless steel skillet, but the thermal conductivity is completely uneven. They claim it has an aluminum core, but if you look at the heat distribution patterns when you deglaze the pan, it's obvious the cladding is way too thin on the edges. You end up with these massive hot spots right in the center, which completely ruins the temperature control needed for proper protein denaturation. It's like the manufacturer didn't even consult a basic thermodynamics textbook before putting it into production. I actually wrote a very detailed review explaining how they could improve the heat capacity by just increasing the thickness of the aluminum layer by a few millimeters, but I'm sure it just got buried under a bunch of fake five-star reviews. People just don't care about structural integrity anymore. \n\nBut, um... yeah. To answer your question... keeping up with things is definitely really hard right now. Between work and home, I just feel like I'm dropping the ball on everything.\n\nDoctor: Is there any aspect of your life where you feel completely free from worrying?\n\nPatient: Um, completely free from it? No, I don't... I don't think so. It's pretty much always there, buzzing in the background, you know? Even when I'm trying to relax, it just creeps in. \n\nLike, I tried to unwind last weekend by setting up my new aquarium. I thought, okay, fish are supposed to be calming, right? But the filtration system I bought was an absolute joke. They advertised it as a multi-stage biological filter, but the flow rate through the ceramic media is way too fast for any meaningful colonization of nitrifying bacteria. The residence time of the water in the filter chamber is so short that the ammonia conversion rate is practically negligible. And the impeller design! It's supposed to be magnetic drive, but the ceramic shaft has this microscopic wobble that creates a low-frequency hum that resonates through the entire glass tank. It's a basic mechanical engineering failure—if they had just used a slightly denser polymer for the housing or added a silicone dampening ring, it would have eliminated the vibration entirely. I ended up spending six hours reverse-engineering the pump housing and 3D printing a custom baffle just to get the flow dynamics right. It's just exhausting that you can't buy a single consumer product that actually adheres to basic fluid dynamics principles. \n\nBut, uh, sorry, what was the question? Oh, being free from worrying. Yeah, no, I don't really have any part of my life where it just turns off. It's pretty constant.\n\nDoctor: Are there any situations or places that you avoid because of your worrying?\n\nPatient: Avoid places? Um, no, not really. I don't think I avoid going anywhere specific because of it. Like, I still go out to cafes and stuff to try and get work done. \n\nThough, I went to this new coffee shop yesterday, and I was absolutely appalled by the takeaway cups they were using. They had these cheap extruded polystyrene lids, which is just... I mean, it's completely irresponsible. When exposed to aqueous solutions above 85 degrees Celsius, the structural integrity of that specific polymer matrix starts to degrade. You risk leaching styrene monomers directly into the beverage! They had a little sign claiming it was high-impact polystyrene, but the glass transition temperature is clearly way too low for the thermal load of a freshly brewed Americano. I was sitting there, and I lightly scraped the inside of the lid with a stirrer, and you could literally feel how the heat had compromised the polymer chains. I actually ended up trying to explain to the manager that they need to switch to a polypropylene or at least a crystallized PET lid if they want to prevent the mass transfer of volatile organic compounds into their customers' drinks. It's basic polymer science, but they just looked at me like I was speaking a different language. It drives me crazy how little thought goes into food-safe material selection.\n\nBut, um... sorry, I lost my train of thought. Avoiding places? Yeah, no, I don't avoid anywhere. I just kind of deal with the worrying wherever I am.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, yeah. Thanks. Um, I came in because... well, my mind is just constantly jumping around. Like, non-stop. I work in a materials lab—mostly chemistry stuff—and we just got this new partnership. Which, by the way, the guys from the new company came in last week for a tour and they were wearing these ridiculous bulky suits right near the clean area, which makes zero sense if you know literally anything about particulate contamination. I mean, the airflow in there is specifically calibrated, so bringing in outside fabric like that is just asking for a compromised batch. \n\nBut anyway, sorry... seeing them just set me off, and now I can't stop thinking about whether this new partnership is going to cut my position. And then I start panicking that maybe my data is wrong, and if they fire me, my bills are just going to pile up. My family already thinks I'm falling behind since I only have some college credits and haven't finished my degree yet, and... I don't know, I just keep spiraling, thinking I've already completely ruined my chances at ever getting into grad school. It's just all of that, all the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any episodes where you felt an intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I don't think so? It's more like a constant hum in the background, you know? Not like a sudden spike. Speaking of spikes, we actually had a massive power surge at the lab on Tuesday. Completely fried one of the older mass spectrometers. Which, honestly, I've been telling my supervisor for months that we needed to update the surge protectors in that wing, but nobody listens to the guy who hasn't finished his degree yet, right? And now they're looking at a $40,000 repair bill, which they're probably going to blame on my department's budget somehow. But yeah, no... I don't get those sudden, intense peaks of fear or whatever. Just... always worried.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding any situations or activities because of your worries?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I avoid things. I still go to work every day, I still do what I need to do. I mean, I have to, right? If I don't show up, who's going to calibrate the equipment? Definitely not Greg. Greg is this guy in my department who literally just started, and yesterday I caught him trying to clean a beaker with regular tap water. Tap water! Do you know how many dissolved minerals are in the city water here? It's absurd. I had to spend like twenty minutes explaining to him why we use deionized water, and he just looked at me like I was speaking a different language. It's so frustrating because if he messes up a sample, it's going to fall on me since I'm the one who logged the batch. And then my supervisor will use that as an excuse to not sign off on my tuition reimbursement for next semester, which I absolutely need if I'm ever going to finish my degree. But no, to answer your question, I don't avoid situations. I just... I'm there, but I'm just worrying the whole time I'm doing them.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain behaviors or mental acts over and over?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I mean, I don't have to count things or wash my hands a million times or anything like that. Although, speaking of washing hands, the soap they use in the lab bathrooms is honestly terrible. It's this cheap pink stuff that completely strips the oils from your skin. I brought in my own unscented glycerin soap last week, and someone actually complained about it. Can you believe that? They complained that my soap didn't have a scent. Meanwhile, half the lab is walking around smelling like artificial cherry blossom, which is a massive issue when we're running sensory panels on the new polymer coatings. The volatile organic compounds from their cheap perfumes totally skew the baseline readings. I tried explaining this in the weekly meeting, but my supervisor just rolled his eyes. It's like they want the data to be flawed. And if the data is flawed, the new partnership goes under, and then I'm definitely out of a job. But yeah, no, I don't repeat behaviors or anything.\n\nDoctor: Are your worries mostly about one thing, like work or school, or are they spread across many different areas of your life?\n\nPatient: Oh, it's definitely spread out. My mind is just constantly jumping around from one thing to the next. Like, I'll be worrying about whether our lab's new partnership is going to end up cutting my position. Which, speaking of that partnership, the corporate reps from the other company came in for a tour on Thursday, and one of them was wearing these ridiculous suede loafers. In a wet lab! Suede! Do you know how porous suede is? If someone spilled even a drop of the toluene we were working with, it would soak right through to his skin in a fraction of a second. I tried to point out the safety hazard to my supervisor, but he just shushed me because they're the ones bringing in the funding. It's completely insane to ignore basic OSHA protocols just because a guy has a nice watch. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, I'll be worrying about losing my job to that merger, and then suddenly my brain switches to agonizing over whether my data from the morning batches are wrong. And then I think, if my data is wrong and I lose my job, my bills are going to pile up. I just got my electric bill yesterday and they raised the delivery fee again, which is a total scam. They don't even explain what the delivery fee actually covers on the statement, it's just a flat rate that keeps going up. So I'm stressing about the bills, and then that makes me think about how my family already thinks I'm falling behind everyone else my age, and I start panicking about whether I've already completely ruined my chances at ever getting into grad school. \n\nSo, no, it's not just work or school. It's... it's literally everything. It never really stops.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is interfering with your ability to enjoy daily activities or function normally?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I... I guess it is. It's definitely getting in the way of things. Like, I completely missed an internal lab deadline last week because I just couldn't get my writing and my data review organized. My brain was just buzzing too much. Which, speaking of the data review, the new software they made us install for the spectroscopy analysis is an absolute nightmare. The user interface looks like it was designed in 1998, and it keeps crashing every time I try to export the raw files to Excel. I put in a ticket with IT three days ago, and you know what they told me? They said to \"try restarting the computer.\" As if I haven't tried that a dozen times already! It's infuriating. They get paid to sit in that air-conditioned basement and they can't even troubleshoot a basic compatibility issue. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, missing that deadline was bad. And now it feels like even just the regular, everyday stuff is too much. Preparing the routine reports, showing up for these weekly art nights my friends do, even just cooking a meal for my family... it all just feels like this massive mountain I can't climb. I'm just too exhausted from worrying all the time to actually keep up with any of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is causing you significant distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it really is. I mean, like I was just saying, it's causing so much distress that I'm literally dropping the ball on everything. Missing that internal lab deadline was a huge deal. I just couldn't get my writing and my data review organized because my head was spinning. Which, honestly, part of the reason I couldn't get it organized is because my desk is right next to the main walkway in the lab, and people are constantly walking by dragging their feet. There's this one guy, Dave, from the synthesis team, and he wears these heavy work boots that scuff against the linoleum every single time he takes a step. *Scuff, scuff, scuff.* It's maddening. I asked him once if he could maybe pick his feet up a little, and he looked at me like I was the crazy one. But how are you supposed to concentrate on complex data review when someone is essentially sandpapering the floor right behind your head? \n\nBut anyway, yeah, the distress is just... it's overwhelming. It's not just the big deadlines, either. It's gotten to the point where even routine responsibilities feel impossible. Like preparing the standard weekly reports, or showing up for those art nights my friends do on Tuesdays, or even just cooking a decent meal for my family. It all just feels like way too much to keep up with. I'm just so drained by the constant worrying that I can't handle the normal stuff anymore.\n\nDoctor: Is there any part of your life where you feel your worrying is less or not present at all?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don't think there's anywhere it just... shuts off. Even when I try to force myself to take a break, it doesn't work. Like, yesterday I went into the staff breakroom just to sit and stare at the wall for five minutes and try to clear my head. But as soon as I sat down, I noticed someone had left the microwave door wide open. Which, first of all, leaves the little interior light on, and that just wastes the bulb's lifespan for literally no reason. And second, I could see splattered red sauce all over the ceiling of the inside. Who microwaves pasta without putting a paper towel over the bowl? It's basic thermodynamics! The water in the sauce heats up, expands into steam, and pops the food everywhere. It's so completely disrespectful to a communal space. \n\nI ended up spending my whole quote-unquote break scrubbing baked-on marinara off the rotating glass plate because I couldn't stand looking at it. And then I started stressing that we're going to get ants in the breakroom, which would be an absolute disaster considering we work in a lab setting where cross-contamination is a huge liability. \n\nSo... yeah. To answer your question, no. I'm always on edge. There isn't really a time or place where the worrying isn't there.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding that your worrying is affecting your relationships with friends and family?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean, I think I mentioned this a minute ago, but it really is. Like I was saying, I've just been too overwhelmed to even cook a basic meal for my family, and I've completely stopped showing up for those Tuesday art nights my friends put together. Which, honestly, maybe it's for the best because the last time I went, somebody brought these incredibly cheap acrylic paints. I'm talking bottom-of-the-barrel, discount-store stuff. The pigment separation was just atrocious. As someone who actually studies materials, looking at that emulsion breakdown just drove me completely insane. You can't just dump tap water into a polymer binder and expect it to adhere to a canvas without flaking! There's a specific cross-linking process that has to happen for the film to form properly. I tried explaining the basic chemical properties to them, but they just wanted to paint their little poorly-blended sunsets. It's just so frustrating when people don't respect the materials they're working with. \n\nBut... wait, what was I saying? Oh, right, my relationships. Yeah, I just can't keep up with them right now. It all feels like too much. Just like how I missed that internal lab deadline because I couldn't get my writing and data review organized. I'm just dropping the ball on everything—my friends, my family, even routine stuff like preparing those standard lab reports. I just can't keep up with any of it anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any physical symptoms like headaches, stomachaches, or difficulty sleeping because of your worrying?\n\nPatient: Headaches or stomachaches? Um, no, I don't think so. I haven't really noticed anything like that. And sleeping... I mean, I guess I sleep okay? Honestly, the only thing that really messes with my sleep is the HVAC unit outside my apartment window. \n\nIt vibrates at this incredibly specific low frequency, like right around 60 hertz, which is just the exact pitch to resonate perfectly with my single-pane glass window. It's a basic acoustic coupling issue. If the property management company would just install a proper elastomeric isolation pad under the condenser unit, it would dampen the vibration completely. I even printed out a spec sheet for a neoprene mount that costs literally twelve dollars and taped it to the manager's door, but they just totally ignored it. It's just willful ignorance of basic physics. You can't just bolt a heavy, unbalanced rotary compressor directly to a concrete slab next to a residential wall and expect the kinetic energy to just magically disappear into the ether. It has to transfer somewhere! It just drives me crazy when people refuse to implement simple, structurally sound solutions. \n\nBut... wait, sorry. What was the question? Oh, right, physical stuff from the worrying. Yeah, no, I don't really get stomachaches or headaches or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding that your worrying is significantly impacting your ability to perform your job or maintain your role at the lab?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... I think I brought this up a few minutes ago, actually. It really is impacting my job. Like I said, I completely missed an internal lab deadline recently because I just couldn't get my writing and data review organized. And it's not even just the big deadlines, it's the routine stuff, too. Even just preparing standard lab reports feels completely overwhelming right now. \n\nWhich is ridiculous because the reporting software we use is supposed to streamline the process, but the user interface is an absolute nightmare. The data visualization module defaults to these absurd, neon color palettes that completely distort the visual hierarchy of the graphs. Like, you can't just put a high-saturation yellow next to a bright cyan on a scatter plot and expect the human eye to accurately process the data point density! It creates this optical illusion called simultaneous contrast where the colors vibrate against each other, making the data look completely skewed. I submitted a ticket to IT explaining how they could just change the default CSS to a colorblind-friendly, sequential palette, which would take them maybe five minutes of coding. But of course, they closed the ticket without even reading it. It's just so infuriating when people don't understand basic visual processing principles. \n\nBut, um... sorry. To get back to your question... yes. The worrying is definitely impacting my job. Between missing that deadline and struggling with the reports... and like I said before, even stuff outside of work, like cooking for my family or going to those art nights. It all just feels like way too much to keep up with.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding that your worrying is making it difficult for you to make decisions or take actions in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Making decisions? Um, no, I don't really think so. I haven't noticed any issues with that. Like, I just had to decide on a new dish soap at the store yesterday, and I just picked one out. \n\nWell, I say I just picked one, but honestly, looking at the options was infuriating because the commercial surfactant blends they use now are completely illogical. They market all these \"eco-friendly\" formulas, but if you actually look at the molecular structure of the primary cleansing agents, they've just substituted standard sulfates with a decyl glucoside that doesn't even have the proper hydrophilic-lipophilic balance to break down basic cooking oils. You end up having to use three times as much product to achieve the same micelle formation! Which completely negates any environmental benefit because you're just dumping massive amounts of excess non-ionic surfactants into the municipal wastewater system. It's just blatant greenwashing masking incredibly lazy organic chemistry. I literally had to stand in the aisle pulling up safety data sheets on my phone because I refuse to buy a product formulated by someone who doesn't understand basic lipid emulsion. \n\nBut... wait, what did you ask me? Oh, right, struggling to make decisions. Yeah, no, I don't really have a problem with that. I usually just figure out what needs to be done and do it.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding that your worrying is making it hard for you to keep up with your responsibilities or maintain relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... I feel like we just talked about this, right? But yes, it really is. Like I said a minute ago, I completely missed an internal lab deadline recently. I just couldn't get my writing and data review organized at all. And it's bleeding into everything else. Even routine stuff, like preparing those lab reports, or trying to cook a decent meal for my family, or just showing up for those art nights I usually go to... it all just feels like way too much to keep up with. \n\nWhich is frustrating about the cooking, especially, because I actually really enjoy the Maillard reaction when you get a perfect sear on a piece of protein. But lately, the quality of the cookware I've been using has been driving me insane. I bought this supposedly \"high-end\" stainless steel skillet, but the thermal conductivity is completely uneven. They claim it has an aluminum core, but if you look at the heat distribution patterns when you deglaze the pan, it's obvious the cladding is way too thin on the edges. You end up with these massive hot spots right in the center, which completely ruins the temperature control needed for proper protein denaturation. It's like the manufacturer didn't even consult a basic thermodynamics textbook before putting it into production. I actually wrote a very detailed review explaining how they could improve the heat capacity by just increasing the thickness of the aluminum layer by a few millimeters, but I'm sure it just got buried under a bunch of fake five-star reviews. People just don't care about structural integrity anymore. \n\nBut, um... yeah. To answer your question... keeping up with things is definitely really hard right now. Between work and home, I just feel like I'm dropping the ball on everything.\n\nDoctor: Is there any aspect of your life where you feel completely free from worrying?\n\nPatient: Um, completely free from it? No, I don't... I don't think so. It's pretty much always there, buzzing in the background, you know? Even when I'm trying to relax, it just creeps in. \n\nLike, I tried to unwind last weekend by setting up my new aquarium. I thought, okay, fish are supposed to be calming, right? But the filtration system I bought was an absolute joke. They advertised it as a multi-stage biological filter, but the flow rate through the ceramic media is way too fast for any meaningful colonization of nitrifying bacteria. The residence time of the water in the filter chamber is so short that the ammonia conversion rate is practically negligible. And the impeller design! It's supposed to be magnetic drive, but the ceramic shaft has this microscopic wobble that creates a low-frequency hum that resonates through the entire glass tank. It's a basic mechanical engineering failure—if they had just used a slightly denser polymer for the housing or added a silicone dampening ring, it would have eliminated the vibration entirely. I ended up spending six hours reverse-engineering the pump housing and 3D printing a custom baffle just to get the flow dynamics right. It's just exhausting that you can't buy a single consumer product that actually adheres to basic fluid dynamics principles. \n\nBut, uh, sorry, what was the question? Oh, being free from worrying. Yeah, no, I don't really have any part of my life where it just turns off. It's pretty constant.\n\nDoctor: Are there any situations or places that you avoid because of your worrying?\n\nPatient: Avoid places? Um, no, not really. I don't think I avoid going anywhere specific because of it. Like, I still go out to cafes and stuff to try and get work done. \n\nThough, I went to this new coffee shop yesterday, and I was absolutely appalled by the takeaway cups they were using. They had these cheap extruded polystyrene lids, which is just... I mean, it's completely irresponsible. When exposed to aqueous solutions above 85 degrees Celsius, the structural integrity of that specific polymer matrix starts to degrade. You risk leaching styrene monomers directly into the beverage! They had a little sign claiming it was high-impact polystyrene, but the glass transition temperature is clearly way too low for the thermal load of a freshly brewed Americano. I was sitting there, and I lightly scraped the inside of the lid with a stirrer, and you could literally feel how the heat had compromised the polymer chains. I actually ended up trying to explain to the manager that they need to switch to a polypropylene or at least a crystallized PET lid if they want to prevent the mass transfer of volatile organic compounds into their customers' drinks. It's basic polymer science, but they just looked at me like I was speaking a different language. It drives me crazy how little thought goes into food-safe material selection.\n\nBut, um... sorry, I lost my train of thought. Avoiding places? Yeah, no, I don't avoid anywhere. I just kind of deal with the worrying wherever I am.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding that your worrying is significantly impacting your ability to function at work or in social situations?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... I feel like we just talked about this, right? But yes, it really is. Like I said a minute ago, I completely missed an internal lab deadline recently. I just couldn't get my writing and data review organized at all. And it's bleeding into everything else. Even routine stuff, like preparing those lab reports, or trying to cook a decent meal for my family, or just showing up for those art nights I usually go to... it all just feels like way too much to keep up with. \n\nWhich is frustrating about the cooking, especially, because I actually really enjoy the Maillard reaction when you get a perfect sear on a piece of protein. They claim it has an aluminum core, but if you look at the heat distribution patterns when you deglaze the pan, it's obvious the cladding is way too thin on the edges. You end up with these massive hot spots right in the center, which completely ruins the temperature control needed for proper protein denaturation. It's like the manufacturer didn't even consult a basic thermodynamics textbook before putting it into production. I actually wrote a very detailed review explaining how they could improve the heat capacity by just increasing the thickness of the aluminum layer by a few millimeters, but I'm sure it just got buried under a bunch of fake five-star reviews. People just don't care about structural integrity anymore. \n\nBut, um... yeah. To answer your question... keeping up with things is definitely really hard right now. Between work and home, I just feel like I'm dropping the ball on everything.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. And second, I could see splattered red sauce all over the ceiling of the inside. Who microwaves pasta without putting a paper towel over the bowl? It's basic thermodynamics! The water in the sauce heats up, expands into steam, and pops the food everywhere. It's so completely disrespectful to a communal space. \n\nI ended up spending my whole quote-unquote break scrubbing baked-on marinara off the rotating glass plate because I couldn't stand looking at it. And then I started stressing that we're going to get ants in the breakroom, which would be an absolute disaster considering we work in a lab setting where cross-contamination is a huge liability. \n\nSo... yeah. To answer your question, no. I'm always on edge. There isn't really a time or place where the worrying isn't there.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding that your worrying is affecting your relationships with friends and family?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean, I think I mentioned this a minute ago, but it really is. Like I was saying, I've just been too overwhelmed to even cook a basic meal for my family, and I've completely stopped showing up for those Tuesday art nights my friends put together. Which, honestly, maybe it's for the best because the last time I went, somebody brought these incredibly cheap acrylic paints. I'm talking bottom-of-the-barrel, discount-store stuff. The pigment separation was just atrocious. As someone who actually studies materials, looking at that emulsion breakdown just drove me completely insane. You can't just dump tap water into a polymer binder and expect it to adhere to a canvas without flaking! There's a specific cross-linking process that has to happen for the film to form properly. I tried explaining the basic chemical properties to them, but they just wanted to paint their little poorly-blended sunsets. It's just so frustrating when people don't respect the materials they're working with. \n\nBut... wait, what was I saying? Oh, right, my relationships. Yeah, I just can't keep up with them right now. It all feels like too much. Just like how I missed that internal lab deadline because I couldn't get my writing and data review organized. I'm just dropping the ball on everything—my friends, my family, even routine stuff like preparing those standard lab reports. I just can't keep up with any of it anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any physical symptoms like headaches, stomachaches, or difficulty sleeping because of your worrying?\n\nPatient: Headaches or stomachaches? Um, no, I don't think so. I haven't really noticed anything like that. And sleeping... I mean, I guess I sleep okay? Honestly, the only thing that really messes with my sleep is the HVAC unit outside my apartment window. \n\nIt vibrates at this incredibly specific low frequency, like right around 60 hertz, which is just the exact pitch to resonate perfectly with my single-pane glass window. It's a basic acoustic coupling issue. If the property management company would just install a proper elastomeric isolation pad under the condenser unit, it would dampen the vibration completely. I even printed out a spec sheet for a neoprene mount that costs literally twelve dollars and taped it to the manager's door, but they just totally ignored it. It's just willful ignorance of basic physics. You can't just bolt a heavy, unbalanced rotary compressor directly to a concrete slab next to a residential wall and expect the kinetic energy to just magically disappear into the ether. It has to transfer somewhere! It just drives me crazy when people refuse to implement simple, structurally sound solutions. \n\nBut... wait, sorry. What was the question? Oh, right, physical stuff from the worrying. Yeah, no, I don't really get stomachaches or headaches or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding that your worrying is significantly impacting your ability to perform your job or maintain your role at the lab?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... I think I brought this up a few minutes ago, actually. It really is impacting my job. Like I said, I completely missed an internal lab deadline recently because I just couldn't get my writing and data review organized. And it's not even just the big deadlines, it's the routine stuff, too. Even just preparing standard lab reports feels completely overwhelming right now. \n\nWhich is ridiculous because the reporting software we use is supposed to streamline the process, but the user interface is an absolute nightmare. The data visualization module defaults to these absurd, neon color palettes that completely distort the visual hierarchy of the graphs. Like, you can't just put a high-saturation yellow next to a bright cyan on a scatter plot and expect the human eye to accurately process the data point density! It creates this optical illusion called simultaneous contrast where the colors vibrate against each other, making the data look completely skewed. I submitted a ticket to IT explaining how they could just change the default CSS to a colorblind-friendly, sequential palette, which would take them maybe five minutes of coding. But of course, they closed the ticket without even reading it. It's just so infuriating when people don't understand basic visual processing principles. \n\nBut, um... sorry. To get back to your question... yes. The worrying is definitely impacting my job. Between missing that deadline and struggling with the reports... and like I said before, even stuff outside of work, like cooking for my family or going to those art nights. It all just feels like way too much to keep up with.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding that your worrying is making it difficult for you to make decisions or take actions in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Making decisions? Um, no, I don't really think so. I haven't noticed any issues with that. Like, I just had to decide on a new dish soap at the store yesterday, and I just picked one out. \n\nWell, I say I just picked one, but honestly, looking at the options was infuriating because the commercial surfactant blends they use now are completely illogical. They market all these \"eco-friendly\" formulas, but if you actually look at the molecular structure of the primary cleansing agents, they've just substituted standard sulfates with a decyl glucoside that doesn't even have the proper hydrophilic-lipophilic balance to break down basic cooking oils. You end up having to use three times as much product to achieve the same micelle formation! Which completely negates any environmental benefit because you're just dumping massive amounts of excess non-ionic surfactants into the municipal wastewater system. It's just blatant greenwashing masking incredibly lazy organic chemistry. I literally had to stand in the aisle pulling up safety data sheets on my phone because I refuse to buy a product formulated by someone who doesn't understand basic lipid emulsion. \n\nBut... wait, what did you ask me? Oh, right, struggling to make decisions. Yeah, no, I don't really have a problem with that. I usually just figure out what needs to be done and do it.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding that your worrying is making it hard for you to keep up with your responsibilities or maintain relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... I feel like we just talked about this, right? But yes, it really is. Like I said a minute ago, I completely missed an internal lab deadline recently. I just couldn't get my writing and data review organized at all. And it's bleeding into everything else. Even routine stuff, like preparing those lab reports, or trying to cook a decent meal for my family, or just showing up for those art nights I usually go to... it all just feels like way too much to keep up with. \n\nWhich is frustrating about the cooking, especially, because I actually really enjoy the Maillard reaction when you get a perfect sear on a piece of protein. But lately, the quality of the cookware I've been using has been driving me insane. I bought this supposedly \"high-end\" stainless steel skillet, but the thermal conductivity is completely uneven. They claim it has an aluminum core, but if you look at the heat distribution patterns when you deglaze the pan, it's obvious the cladding is way too thin on the edges. You end up with these massive hot spots right in the center, which completely ruins the temperature control needed for proper protein denaturation. It's like the manufacturer didn't even consult a basic thermodynamics textbook before putting it into production. I actually wrote a very detailed review explaining how they could improve the heat capacity by just increasing the thickness of the aluminum layer by a few millimeters, but I'm sure it just got buried under a bunch of fake five-star reviews. People just don't care about structural integrity anymore. \n\nBut, um... yeah. To answer your question... keeping up with things is definitely really hard right now. Between work and home, I just feel like I'm dropping the ball on everything.\n\nDoctor: Is there any aspect of your life where you feel completely free from worrying?\n\nPatient: Um, completely free from it? No, I don't... I don't think so. It's pretty much always there, buzzing in the background, you know? Even when I'm trying to relax, it just creeps in. \n\nLike, I tried to unwind last weekend by setting up my new aquarium. I thought, okay, fish are supposed to be calming, right? But the filtration system I bought was an absolute joke. They advertised it as a multi-stage biological filter, but the flow rate through the ceramic media is way too fast for any meaningful colonization of nitrifying bacteria. The residence time of the water in the filter chamber is so short that the ammonia conversion rate is practically negligible. And the impeller design! It's supposed to be magnetic drive, but the ceramic shaft has this microscopic wobble that creates a low-frequency hum that resonates through the entire glass tank. It's a basic mechanical engineering failure—if they had just used a slightly denser polymer for the housing or added a silicone dampening ring, it would have eliminated the vibration entirely. I ended up spending six hours reverse-engineering the pump housing and 3D printing a custom baffle just to get the flow dynamics right. It's just exhausting that you can't buy a single consumer product that actually adheres to basic fluid dynamics principles. \n\nBut, uh, sorry, what was the question? Oh, being free from worrying. Yeah, no, I don't really have any part of my life where it just turns off. It's pretty constant.\n\nDoctor: Are there any situations or places that you avoid because of your worrying?\n\nPatient: Avoid places? Um, no, not really. I don't think I avoid going anywhere specific because of it. Like, I still go out to cafes and stuff to try and get work done. \n\nThough, I went to this new coffee shop yesterday, and I was absolutely appalled by the takeaway cups they were using. They had these cheap extruded polystyrene lids, which is just... I mean, it's completely irresponsible. When exposed to aqueous solutions above 85 degrees Celsius, the structural integrity of that specific polymer matrix starts to degrade. You risk leaching styrene monomers directly into the beverage! They had a little sign claiming it was high-impact polystyrene, but the glass transition temperature is clearly way too low for the thermal load of a freshly brewed Americano. I was sitting there, and I lightly scraped the inside of the lid with a stirrer, and you could literally feel how the heat had compromised the polymer chains. I actually ended up trying to explain to the manager that they need to switch to a polypropylene or at least a crystallized PET lid if they want to prevent the mass transfer of volatile organic compounds into their customers' drinks. It's basic polymer science, but they just looked at me like I was speaking a different language. It drives me crazy how little thought goes into food-safe material selection.\n\nBut, um... sorry, I lost my train of thought. Avoiding places? Yeah, no, I don't avoid anywhere. I just kind of deal with the worrying wherever I am.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding that your worrying is significantly impacting your ability to function at work or in social situations?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... I feel like we literally just went over this... didn't we? But, yeah. Yes. It's interfering with basically everything. Like I was just saying, I completely blew past an internal lab deadline recently. I just... I couldn't get my writing and the data review organized. My brain was just spinning. I can't keep up. 

And honestly, speaking of the lab reports, the new software they have us using for data visualization is a complete disaster. It's supposed to streamline our workflow, right? But the rendering engine they built it on is so poorly optimized. When you try to plot a simple three-dimensional scatter graph of molecular interactions, the memory leak is so severe it practically crashes the entire workstation. They get paid to sit in that air-conditioned basement and they can't even troubleshoot a basic compatibility issue. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, missing that deadline was bad. And now it feels like even just the regular, everyday stuff is too much. Preparing the routine reports, showing up for these weekly art nights my friends do, even just cooking a meal for my family... it all just feels like this massive mountain I can't climb. I'm just too exhausted from worrying all the time to actually keep up with any of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is causing you significant distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it really is. I mean, like I was just saying, it's causing so much distress that I'm literally dropping the ball on everything. Missing that internal lab deadline was a huge deal. I just couldn't get my writing and my data review organized because my head was spinning. Which, honestly, part of the reason I couldn't get it organized is because my desk is right next to the main walkway in the lab, and people are constantly walking by dragging their feet. There's this one guy, Dave, from the synthesis team, and he wears these heavy work boots that scuff against the linoleum every single time he takes a step. *Scuff, scuff, scuff.* It's maddening. I asked him once if he could maybe pick his feet up a little, and he looked at me like I was the crazy one. But how are you supposed to concentrate on complex data review when someone is essentially sandpapering the floor right behind your head? \n\nBut anyway, yeah, the distress is just... it's overwhelming. It's not just the big deadlines, either. It's gotten to the point where even routine responsibilities feel impossible. Like preparing the standard weekly reports, or showing up for those art nights my friends do on Tuesdays, or even just cooking a decent meal for my family. It all just feels like way too much to keep up with. I'm just so drained by the constant worrying that I can't handle the normal stuff anymore.\n\nDoctor: Is there any part of your life where you feel your worrying is less or not present at all?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don't think there's anywhere it just... shuts off. Even when I try to force myself to take a break, it doesn't work. Like, yesterday I went into the staff breakroom just to sit and stare at the wall for five minutes and try to clear my head. But as soon as I sat down, I noticed someone had left the microwave door wide open. Which, first of all, leaves the little interior light on, and that just wastes the bulb's lifespan for literally no reason. And second, I could see splattered red sauce all over the ceiling of the inside. Who microwaves pasta without putting a paper towel over the bowl? It's basic thermodynamics! The water in the sauce heats up, expands into steam, and pops the food everywhere. It's so completely disrespectful to a communal space. \n\nI ended up spending my whole quote-unquote break scrubbing baked-on marinara off the rotating glass plate because I couldn't stand looking at it. And then I started stressing that we're going to get ants in the breakroom, which would be an absolute disaster considering we work in a lab setting where cross-contamination is a huge liability. \n\nSo... yeah. To answer your question, no. I'm always on edge. There isn't really a time or place where the worrying isn't there.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding that your worrying is affecting your relationships with friends and family?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean, I think I mentioned this a minute ago, but it really is. Like I was saying, I've just been too overwhelmed to even cook a basic meal for my family, and I've completely stopped showing up for those Tuesday art nights my friends put together. Which, honestly, maybe it's for the best because the last time I went, somebody brought these incredibly cheap acrylic paints. I'm talking bottom-of-the-barrel, discount-store stuff. The pigment separation was just atrocious. As someone who actually studies materials, looking at that emulsion breakdown just drove me completely insane. You can't just dump tap water into a polymer binder and expect it to adhere to a canvas without flaking! There's a specific cross-linking process that has to happen for the film to form properly. I tried explaining the basic chemical properties to them, but they just wanted to paint their little poorly-blended sunsets. It's just so frustrating when people don't respect the materials they're working with. \n\nBut... wait, what was I saying? Oh, right, my relationships. Yeah, I just can't keep up with them right now. It all feels like too much. Just like how I missed that internal lab deadline because I couldn't get my writing and data review organized. I'm just dropping the ball on everything—my friends, my family, even routine stuff like preparing those standard lab reports. I just can't keep up with any of it anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any physical symptoms like headaches, stomachaches, or difficulty sleeping because of your worrying?\n\nPatient: Headaches or stomachaches? Um, no, I don't think so. I haven't really noticed anything like that. And sleeping... I mean, I guess I sleep okay? Honestly, the only thing that really messes with my sleep is the HVAC unit outside my apartment window. \n\nIt vibrates at this incredibly specific low frequency, like right around 60 hertz, which is just the exact pitch to resonate perfectly with my single-pane glass window. It's a basic acoustic coupling issue. If the property management company would just install a proper elastomeric isolation pad under the condenser unit, it would dampen the vibration completely. I even printed out a spec sheet for a neoprene mount that costs literally twelve dollars and taped it to the manager's door, but they just totally ignored it. It's just willful ignorance of basic physics. You can't just bolt a heavy, unbalanced rotary compressor directly to a concrete slab next to a residential wall and expect the kinetic energy to just magically disappear into the ether. It has to transfer somewhere! It just drives me crazy when people refuse to implement simple, structurally sound solutions. \n\nBut... wait, sorry. What was the question? Oh, right, physical stuff from the worrying. Yeah, no, I don't really get stomachaches or headaches or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding that your worrying is significantly impacting your ability to perform your job or maintain your role at the lab?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... And it's not even just the big deadlines, it's the routine stuff, too. Even just preparing standard lab reports feels completely overwhelming right now. \n\nWhich is ridiculous because the reporting software we use is supposed to streamline the process, but the user interface is an absolute nightmare. The data visualization module defaults to these absurd, neon color palettes that completely distort the visual hierarchy of the graphs. Like, you can't just put a high-saturation yellow next to a bright cyan on a scatter plot and expect the human eye to accurately process the data point density! It creates this optical illusion called simultaneous contrast where the colors vibrate against each other, making the data look completely skewed. I submitted a ticket to IT explaining how they could just change the default CSS to a colorblind-friendly, sequential palette, which would take them maybe five minutes of coding. But of course, they closed the ticket without even reading it. It's just so infuriating when people don't understand basic visual processing principles. \n\nBut, um... sorry. It all just feels like way too much to keep up with.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding that your worrying is making it difficult for you to make decisions or take actions in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Making decisions? Um, no, I don't really think so. I haven't noticed any issues with that. Like, I just had to decide on a new dish soap at the store yesterday, and I just picked one out. \n\nWell, I say I just picked one, but honestly, looking at the options was infuriating because the commercial surfactant blends they use now are completely illogical. They market all these \"eco-friendly\" formulas, but if you actually look at the molecular structure of the primary cleansing agents, they've just substituted standard sulfates with a decyl glucoside that doesn't even have the proper hydrophilic-lipophilic balance to break down basic cooking oils. You end up having to use three times as much product to achieve the same micelle formation! Which completely negates any environmental benefit because you're just dumping massive amounts of excess non-ionic surfactants into the municipal wastewater system. It's just blatant greenwashing masking incredibly lazy organic chemistry. I literally had to stand in the aisle pulling up safety data sheets on my phone because I refuse to buy a product formulated by someone who doesn't understand basic lipid emulsion. \n\nBut... wait, what did you ask me? Oh, right, struggling to make decisions. Yeah, no, I don't really have a problem with that. I usually just figure out what needs to be done and do it.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding that your worrying is making it hard for you to keep up with your responsibilities or maintain relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... I feel like we just talked about this, right? But yes, it really is. Like I said a minute ago, I completely missed an internal lab deadline recently. I just couldn't get my writing and data review organized at all. And it's bleeding into everything else. Even routine stuff, like preparing those lab reports, or trying to cook a decent meal for my family, or just showing up for those art nights I usually go to... it all just feels like way too much to keep up with. \n\nWhich is frustrating about the cooking, especially, because I actually really enjoy the Maillard reaction when you get a perfect sear on a piece of protein. But lately, the quality of the cookware I've been using has been driving me insane. I bought this supposedly \"high-end\" stainless steel skillet, but the thermal conductivity is completely uneven. They claim it has an aluminum core, but if you look at the heat distribution patterns when you deglaze the pan, it's obvious the cladding is way too thin on the edges. You end up with these massive hot spots right in the center, which completely ruins the temperature control needed for proper protein denaturation. People just don't care about structural integrity anymore. \n\nBut, um... yeah. To answer your question... keeping up with things is definitely really hard right now. I ended up spending six hours reverse-engineering the pump housing and 3D printing a custom baffle just to get the flow dynamics right. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, it... it really is. It's just this constant, exhausting weight, you know? Like my chest is always tight and I can't ever just relax. It's really draining to feel like this all the time. 

Which, honestly, being drained is exactly how I felt after trying to calibrate the new spectrophotometer in the lab yesterday. It's supposed to be this top-of-the-line model, but the baseline drift on the UV-Vis detector is completely unacceptable for high-precision work. I ran a blank sample of deionized water, and over just twenty minutes, the absorbance readings fluctuated by like 0.005 units. That might not sound like much, but when you're trying to measure trace concentrations of transition metal complexes, that kind of noise completely ruins your signal-to-noise ratio. I opened up the casing to check the deuterium lamp, and I swear, the optical alignment mirrors were mounted on these cheap plastic brackets instead of invar or at least a decent aluminum alloy. Of course they're going to shift with even the slightest thermal expansion! I ended up having to machine a custom mounting plate out of some scrap brass I had just to stabilize the optics. It's infuriating that a piece of equipment that costs as much as a car is built with such poor mechanical tolerances. 

But, um... sorry. What was I saying? Oh, right. The emotional pain. Yeah, it's... it's a lot. It just feels awful all the time.

Are you... are you serious right now? I feel like I'm stuck in a loop. We literally just talked about this. Like, three times. But, yes. Yes, it is. My quality of life is basically non-existent.
